                                                   180
19.6    Large entropy and stopping
The remaining entropy levels are proportional to the dimension. At these levels an ordinary rare-
event bound cannot simply be multiplied by the initial density bound. We instead argue under the
proposed small energy ratio. The posterior mean estimate then makes the revealed information
small. Relative entropy of the stopped observation laws transfers the ordinary stopping probability
to the tilted law, and bounded spin means control the loss after stopping. This retains order-n
entropy and conflicts with the product estimate.
    Now suppose λn ≤ L ≤ Cn. We prove the stronger estimate D0 (f )/N > n−2/3 L by contradiction.
Thus assume
                                      D0 (f )
                                              ≤ n−2/3 L ≤ Cn1/3 .                             (19.19)
                                        N
The objective is to retain a fixed positive multiple of λn until the product endpoint of this path.
Formula (19.18) would then bound that retained entropy by O(n5/6 ) + O(n−3 ), a contradiction.
    We specify the uniformity required at the start. The small-positive-time statement in Proposi-
tion 17.5, with the noise interface of Lemma 17.4, uses diagnostics of the centered Gaussian noise
and admits every additional shift of normalized norm at most Ct. Its separate low-band condition
is a Gaussian small-ball bound uniform in the center, not a simultaneous event for all centers. More
precisely, on a disorder event independent of f , the noise-only diagnostics fail with conditional
probability o(1), integrated over each fixed interval [t∗ , tb ], and
                                   (                  √           )   ′ 3/2
                            sup Pg ∥PtJ (gt + z)∥ < c n t5/4 ≤ e−c nt .                       (19.20)
                             z∈Rn

Here PtJ = 1{2I−J≤tI} = P√t is the cumulative projection used in Lemma 17.4, with W = J. The
covariance on a band of rank comparable to nt3/2 is comparable to tI; projection onto that band
and the Gaussian ball bound prove (19.20). Translation cannot increase the mass of a centered
Gaussian Euclidean ball. For fixed J the spin is independent of the observation noise. Since its
drift shift has normalized norm O(t) for every X ∈ {−1, 1}n , the deterministic shift allowance and
the uniform-in-center probability give the same o(1) integrated failure bound under every initial
spin law, including f 2 µ/N . One first uses Fubini and Markov for the noise-only diagnostic event to
select the disorder event; no union over the 2n spin centers and no factor eL occurs in this step.
The exclusion-at-zero and matrix-word events are also selected independently of f .
     First fix the large scalar endpoint T and the interaction coefficient below which covariance is
globally bounded. Next choose tb > 0 sufficiently small for Proposition 17.5, and obtain the buffered
ordinary test and its failure rate a > 0 on [tb , T ] and the subsequent weakening interval. These
margins are uniform for all sufficiently small changes of the interaction coefficient from one. Fix a
target integrated drift budget η1 sufficiently small relative to aλ. Choose 0 < t∗ ≪ tb so that Ct∗
fits that budget, and finally choose 1 − β ′ > 0 sufficiently small relative to these fixed scales and t∗ .
     After fixing β ′ , reduce the permitted diagonal enlargement in the stability test to a positive ηstab <
(β ) − 1. The stability implication persists on this smaller diagonal class, and (β ′ )2 (1 + ηstab )2 < 1
   ′ −1

supplies the strict margin required by Proposition 17.1.
     Once the resulting stability margins are known, choose the residual tolerance and finite diagnostic
depth in the square-reweighted mean estimate of Proposition 17.1 to realize the target budget. The
target η1 is distinct from that proposition’s accuracy parameter: the leading constant multiplying
its accuracy may depend on the fixed stability margins. Its remaining constants may be arbitrarily
large but are all fixed before n → ∞. The factor n−1/2 in the posterior estimate will therefore still
make the energy-dependent error vanish under (19.19).
     Start directly with precision t∗ I + (1 − β ′ )J, increase scalar precision to T , and then weaken the
interaction at rate 4I + J for duration β ′ . The endpoint is Tβ′ ′ I + J with Tβ′ ′ = T + 4β ′ , the second

                                                    181
fixed endpoint included in (19.10). Positivity of the initial precision follows from choosing 1 − β ′
sufficiently small after t∗ . For comparing this path with the ordinary critical scalar observation, one
may add an independent increment of precision (1 − β ′ )(4I + J) ≻ 0 and increase scalar noise time
by O(1 − β ′ ). This produces a small normalized field displacement outside an exponentially rare
event, so the buffered ordinary tests remain valid with rate a after a fixed decrease.
    Let sg be the onset of the globally bounded-covariance portion. On the interval [t∗ , tb ], the
uniform noise and small-ball argument just given verifies the stability hypotheses with integrated
failure tending to zero under Qν . On their validity set the tilted mean estimate from Proposition 17.1,
together with (19.7), yields an arbitrarily small integrated bound on EQν |mν − mµ |2n . Indeed the
relevant error is bounded by
                                              Cη        Dµh (f )

and its average is Cη + Oη (n−1/6 ) + Oη (n−1/2 ) by (19.19). This estimate initially controls a first
moment. Since |mν − mµ |n ≤ 2, its square is at most twice its first power, giving the needed
quadratic bound. The integrated failure probability contributes only o(1) by the same uniform
bound.
    For times at least tb , use the fixed buffered ordinary stability test, selected independently of
f before observing the path. Let τ ≤ sg be its first violation, taking τ = tb if it already fails
there and τ = sg if no violation occurs. Up to τ the same integrated tilted-mean bound applies
deterministically on the diagnostic event. Write Qτν , Qτµ for the laws of the observations stopped at

On a path that stops, use the spin norm bound for the remaining portion of the observation. Every
                          √
mean has norm at most n, and the precision rate has bounded norm over a bounded duration. Its
entropy-loss integral is consequently at most Cn. Multiplying by the stopping probability bounds
the averaged cost of these paths. By the constant choices, (19.21) and (19.22) make the total loss
before sg smaller than λn/4. Initially H(0) ≥ L/2 ≥ λn/2, so H(sg ) ≥ cλn. The subsequent
global covariance estimate propagates a fixed fraction to the product endpoint. This is the claimed
contradiction with (19.18).
    All disorder, word, and stability events in this argument have fixed thresholds selected inde-
pendently of f . The only function-dependent objects are the tilted law and the entropy level L.
The proof uses the exponential ordinary failure rate through stopped-path relative entropy, and
therefore remains valid when L is of order n.

19.7    The log-Sobolev bound
We have proved (19.1) in both entropy ranges. Lemma 19.1 now gives the promised functional
inequality.
Theorem 19.3 (Critical log-Sobolev upper bound). For every fixed ρ > 0, with probability tending
to one over the Gaussian couplings, the critical zero-field Gibbs measure satisfies
                     Entµ (f 2 ) ≤ n2/3+ρ D0 (f )    for every f : {−1, 1}n −→ R.              (19.23)
The form is normalized for the generator L =         i=1 (Qi − I), where Qi is conditional expectation
                                                    Pn

given X−i .
Proof. Apply the preceding argument with an exponent allowance strictly smaller than ρ. On
∥J∥ ≤ 3 the value M in Lemma 19.1 is Cn. Its fixed constant and (1 + log M )2 factor are absorbed
in the remaining power of n.

    Section 20 derives the mixing and relaxation bounds from this inequality and combines them
with the companion’s lower bounds. The cap comparison also gives the following direct linear test.
It explains the same worst-start lower exponent with weaker quantifiers.
Remark 19.4 (An independent linear lower test). The static comparison also gives a direct, weaker-in-
quantifiers route to the matching worst-start lower exponent. Fix 0 < ϵ < 1/3 and take p = n−1/3+ϵ .
In the uncapped zero-field spherical model, ∥Pp X∥ ≥ cMp with probability tending to one. To see
this, compare with the Gaussian of precision 2 + ξp2 − J. A spectral band with gaps comparable to
p2 already has this lower bound outside e−ckp . The lower norm-density estimate and the bounded
density of the remaining coordinates transfer it through norm conditioning, when ξ > 0 is sufficiently
small. Haar first moments and the zero-field second-moment comparison in Proposition 15.1 transfer
the conclusion to the cube with high Gibbs probability for typical disorder. Spin symmetry and
rank Pp ≤ Cnp3 imply that some Euclidean unit vector a has

                  Varµ (aT X) ≥ cp−2 ,       D0 (aT X) =       a2i µ[Var(Xi | X−i )] ≤ 1.
                                                           X

                                                           i

Thus the rate-one spectral gap is at most Cp2 . Testing a gap eigenfunction from a state where its
absolute value is maximal gives tmix (1/4) ≥ cp−2 ; the discrete gap is smaller by n. Letting the fixed
ϵ decrease proves the same lower exponents. The realized-start statement and the bounds for every
arbitrarily slowly diverging Mn remain the separate conclusions of Theorem 1.2.


20     From the functional inequalities to mixing
We now turn the two functional endpoints into worst-start mixing bounds. Both deductions use the
unscaled heat-bath form and keep the factor n between the two clocks explicit. The support-profile
proof is the finite-state truncation mechanism of [22, Theorem 1.1 and Lemma 2.1]. The classical
logarithmic Sobolev proof uses the hypercontractive smoothing of [15, Theorems 3.5 and 3.7],
followed by spectral-gap decay.

20.1    The support-profile route

Proof. First obtain a Poincare inequality, which will handle the final part of mixing. Subtract a
median from a real function and take the positive and negative parts f+ , f− . Both supports have

sum of squared increments. The two-point representation of each conditional variance therefore

    If g ≥ 0 is a probability density relative to µ and V = µ(g 2 ), let u = (g − V /4)+ . Markov’s
inequality and the elementary inequality (x − a)2+ ≥ x2 − 2ax for x, a ≥ 0 give

                                   µ(u ̸= 0) ≤ 4/V,        µ(u2 ) ≥ V /2.

The truncation is a contraction, so D(g) ≥ D(u). For V ≥ 8, (20.1) consequently implies

                                            α               αV
                                   D(g) ≥     V log(V /4) ≥    log V.                                (20.4)
                                            2                6
   For the continuous-time semigroup with generator L = ni=1 (Qi − I), where Qi is conditional
                                                                  P

expectation given X−i , the density from a point evolves by gt = etL g0 . Reversibility gives Vt′ =
−2D(gt ). While Vt ≥ 8, setting zt = log(Vt /4) in (20.4) yields zt′ ≤ −αzt . Put

                                                         log(M/4)
                                          t0 = α−1 log            .
                                                            log 2

Since V0 = 1/µ(x) ≤ M , by time t0 we have Vt ≤ 8 for every starting point.                  √
   Afterwards the gap bound gives Vt0 +t − 1 ≤ 7e−2α(log 2)t . The inequality ∥gµ − µ∥TV ≤ 12 V − 1
therefore proves (20.2).
   For attempted updates we obtain the squared-norm decrease directly from positivity of the
average of conditional-expectation projections. The transition operator is P = n−1 i Qi = I + L/n.
                                                                                    P

Each Qi is an orthogonal projection in L2 (µ), hence 0 ⪯ P ⪯ I and P 2 ⪯ P . If gk+1 = P gk , then
                                                                                  1
                     Vk − Vk+1 = ⟨gk , (I − P 2 )gk ⟩µ ≥ ⟨gk , (I − P )gk ⟩µ =      D(gk ).
                                                                                  n
While Vk ≥ 8, this and (20.4) give Vk+1 ≤ Vk [1 − α log(Vk /4)/(2n)].

                                                    184
   If the next value is still at least 8, taking logarithms gives

                     zk+1 ≤ (1 − α/(2n))zk ≤ e−α/(2n) zk ,          zk = log(Vk /4).

The factor is positive: the gap bound and −L ⪯ nI imply α log 2 ≤ n. In addition, whenever this
recurrence is used its bracket must be positive since it bounds the positive value Vk+1 . Thus after
at most k0 steps the squared density norm is at most 8.
    On centered functions the same positive-operator inequality and the gap imply Vk+1 − 1 ≤
(1 − α log 2/n)(Vk − 1). The total-variation inequality above now gives (20.3). This proves the
transfer for deterministic numbers of attempts.
    If log(1/µmin ) ≤ Cn, then log(log(M/4)/ log 2) = OC (log(n + 1)). The two explicit bounds give
the stated big-O consequences for fixed 0 < ε < 1/2. For ε ≥ 1/2, the bound at accuracy 1/4
suffices by monotonicity of mixing time in the accuracy threshold.

    The support bound in Theorem 14.2 holds simultaneously for all tests on its good-disorder event,
so it applies throughout the density evolution in the lemma. Fixed constants and logarithmic losses
are absorbed by choosing a slightly smaller exponent tolerance first. For the big-O consequence used
here, the remaining hypothesis is a lower bound on point masses. On ∥J∥ ≤ 3, the Hamiltonian
oscillation is at most 3n, and
                                    µJ,min ≥ exp[−(3 + log 2)n].                              (20.5)
This proves the asserted worst-start upper exponents through the support-profile route.

20.2    The logarithmic Sobolev route
The classical inequality gives a second, independent smoothing argument. With our convention
Entµ (f 2 ) ≤ CLS D(f ), finite-chain hypercontractivity takes the form

                      etL f          ≤ ∥f ∥p ,   q(t) − 1 = (p − 1)e4t/CLS ,   p > 1.         (20.6)
                              q(t)

Here the factor four is the reversible-chain normalization in [15, Theorem 3.5(ii)]: its energy-
to-entropy constant is 1/CLS for our unscaled form. For the density of a point mass, choose
p = 1 + 1/ max(1, log(1/µmin )). Its Lp norm is at most e. The time at which q(t) = 2 is
O(CLS log(2 + log(1/µmin ))). Expansion of the logarithmic Sobolev inequality at f = 1 + ug, with
µg = 0, gives
                                      2 Varµ (g) ≤ CLS D(g).                               (20.7)
The remaining L2 decay to any fixed TV accuracy therefore costs Oε (CLS ).
    To transfer this bound to attempted updates, compare the two semigroups in L2 . If θ ∈ [0, 1] is
an eigenvalue of P , then θ2k ≤ e−2k(1−θ) . Expanding a centered initial density in an orthonormal
eigenbasis gives
                                  P k (g − 1) ≤ e(k/n)L (g − 1) .                            (20.8)
                                                 2                    2
Thus the same argument, with a factor n in time, proves the discrete upper bound. Using
Theorem 19.3 and (20.5) establishes both upper exponents by the second route as well.




                                                     185
20.3      Sharp exponents and preservation of the lower quantifiers
The linear tests already give lower bounds for the full functional constants: by definition RJ ≥ Rlin,J .
Applying Theorem 1.2 and (20.7) gives

                                  CLS,J ≥ 2RJ ≥ 2Rlin,J = n2/3+o(1)

as a lower-exponent statement in disorder probability. The median consequence of Theorem 14.2
gives the upper bound for RJ , while Theorem 19.3 gives the upper bound for CLS,J (and, by (20.7),
another upper bound for RJ ). Intersecting these finitely many fixed-tolerance disorder events gives
the functional assertions of Theorem 1.1.
    For a lower mixing bound at every fixed threshold below 1/2, choose a nonconstant eigenfunction
g of −LJ with eigenvalue 1/RJ . Center it and normalize ∥g∥∞ = 1. At a point where |g| = 1, its
expectation decays exactly as e−t/RJ , whereas its stationary expectation is zero. Testing TV against
this bounded function gives

                                 max etLJ (x, ·) − µJ              ≥ 12 e−t/RJ .
                                   x                          TV

The corresponding eigenvalue of PJ is 1 − 1/(nRJ ), so the discrete lower bound is 12 (1 − 1/(nRJ ))k .
For every fixed ε < 1/2, these inequalities give mixing lower bounds of order RJ and nRJ , respectively.
Together with the upper bounds, they prove Theorem 1.1.
    The realized-start assertions in Theorem 1.2 apply to every deterministic little-oh time sequence.
Its covariance and linear-Rayleigh assertions apply to every diverging deterministic sequence Mn .
These quantifiers are retained from the companion; no stronger upper quantifier or cutoff statement
is inferred from the new exponent estimates.

20.4      Uniform relative-entropy decay
The spectral-gap and classical logarithmic Sobolev bounds together determine the critical time scale
for uniform relative-entropy decay. We state the normalization explicitly, since constants called
modified logarithmic Sobolev constants use several conventions. For the per-site generator, define

             EJ (u, v) := −µJ [uLJ v] =       µJ [CovµJ (u, v | X−i )] ,           EJ (f, f ) = DJ (f ),
                                          X

                                          i

and let
                                                            EntµJ (g)
                                   CEP,J :=        sup                   .
                                               g>0, µJ g=1 EJ (g, log g)
                                                EntµJ (g)>0

This is the inverse constant for the logarithmic entropy-production inequality. The standard
comparison and semigroup interpretation appear in [9, Propositions 3.5–3.6 and Theorem 2.4]; we
give the short derivation in the present normalization.
Corollary 20.2 (Entropy-production exponent). For every realization of the disorder,
                                          RJ           CLS,J
                                             ≤ CEP,J ≤       .
                                           2            4
Consequently, for the critical zero-field Gaussian model, CEP,J = n2/3+o(1) in disorder probability,
with the fixed exponent-slack meaning of Theorem 1.1. For every initial probability law ν on the
cube and every t ≥ 0,

                    KL(νetLJ ∥µJ ) ≤ e−t/CEP,J KL(ν∥µJ ) ≤ e−4t/CLS,J KL(ν∥µJ ).

                                                     186
                                                     √   √
Proof. For positive a, b, (a − b)(log a − log b) ≥ 4( a − b)2 . Apply this inequality to the two-point
conditional representation of each term of EJ . The classical inequality then gives
                                                  √           4
                             EJ (g, log g) ≥ 4DJ ( g) ≥             EntµJ (g),
                                                            CLS,J

which proves the upper comparison. For a centered real function f , g = 1 + uf is a positive density
for sufficiently small |u|. On the finite cube,

    EntµJ (1 + uf ) = 12 u2 VarµJ (f ) + O(u3 ),      EJ (1 + uf, log(1 + uf )) = u2 DJ (f ) + O(u3 ).

Letting u → 0 and then taking the supremum over f proves CEP,J ≥ RJ /2. For a requested
exponent slack δ, apply Theorem 1.1 with slack δ/2 and then absorb the fixed factors for sufficiently
large n.
    If gt is the density of νetLJ relative to µJ , reversibility gives gt = etLJ g0 and
                                    d
                                       EntµJ (gt ) = −EJ (gt , log gt ).
                                    dt
The chain is irreducible, so gt > 0 for t > 0 even when g0 vanishes at some states. The defining
inequality and Gronwall give the displayed decay; continuity includes t = 0. Conversely, differ-
entiating any uniform exponential entropy bound at zero for positive initial densities recovers its
entropy-production inequality. Thus 1/CEP,J is the optimal rate c in bounds of the displayed form
KL(νetLJ ∥µJ ) ≤ e−ct KL(ν∥µJ ) that hold for every initial law and every time.

    If KL(ν∥µJ ) ≤ H for a fixed H, Pinsker’s inequality therefore gives total-variation accuracy
ε > 0 in OH,ε (CLS,J ) per-site time. A point mass has initial relative entropy log(1/µJ (x)) = O(n)
under (20.5), recovering the logarithmic factor in the worst-start upper bound. The exponent for
CEP,J concerns this uniform rate; it does not assert that every initial law relaxes at the slowest
rate.
